Ladies and gentlemen, Catalan independence undermines the European project by challenging the collective project of Spain. The strength of the Union is based on its integration, never on its segregation, on the desire to share sovereignty in a globalised world in order to regain decision-making power for the benefit of the social majority, especially those who need politics in order to transform their lives. In recent days we have heard from some leaders of independence an unacceptable inflammatory rhetoric, thick messages combined with a demise of functions by the Government of the Generalitat of Catalonia, which are not acceptable to the Government of Spain. And on these issues I would like to make the following three statements. The first, in relation to the Mossos d'Esquadra, is that the Government will not accept any termination of office by the person entrusted with public safety in Catalonia. In the face of recent episodes, last weekend, on some Catalan highways and the unjustifiable inaction of political positions to give orders of action to the Autonomous Police, we have addressed from the Government of Spain to the Government of Catalonia, and above all to those advisers who have competences related to public security, with a clear message and is that they comply with their obligations, and warning that, if they persist in their attitude, a sufficient number of troops from the State Security Forces and Bodies will travel to Catalonia to ensure the security and exercise of the fundamental rights of all Catalans and Catalans. That's how I committed myself to the President of the Generalitat, that's how I committed myself to the Catalan people, and that's how I'm going to enforce the government's word. Ladies and gentlemen, it is not the first Council of Ministers that we are doing outside Madrid and I am already telling you that it is not going to be the last. My will is to bring the Government closer to each and every one of the territories of Spain, recognizing with your presence our esteem for the territorial diversity of the country. [[Rumors]]

ParlaMint-ES_2018-12-12-CD181212.u6	I witnessed the consequences of the civil war in the former Yugoslavia, how the Kosovar refugees fled from the ethnic cleansing perpetrated by dictator Milosevic, as before the Bosnians suffered the same hardships for the hatred of Karadzic and Mladic. I don't need to be told, I don't touch ears, ladies, I saw it and I lived it. I remember with pride the work of the Spanish NGOs, among which there were many Catalan NGOs, I lived with pride how the solidarity of the Spanish people arrived, how Spain received and welcomed many refugees from the civil war in the former Yugoslavia, how the Spanish population turned to support the Bosnian population, with the commitment of Spanish cooperation and hundreds of municipalities, such as the City of Barcelona, ruled by a socialist, by Pasqual Maragall, who precisely rebuilt the library of Sarajevo, and I worked with elbows with them and with the Spanish Army, to which for their dedication and in remembrance of the soldiers who died there dedicated the Plaza de España in the city of Mostar, a square whose walls were crippled by the bullets of nationalist hatred. [[applause]] Years later, ladies and gentlemen, I have returned to Bosnia on more than one occasion and unfortunately, coexistence continues to be an outstanding subject; on the contrary, ethnic segregation is breathed in many of its corners. Moreover, if you have the opportunity to speak with the Bosnians, Croats or Serbs - who also suffered the inclimences and consequences of the civil war - they remember with gratitude the solidarity of Spain, so you don't need to tell me; I saw it and lived it. That is why, ladies and gentlemen, the claim of Catalan independence to the Kosovar or Slovene path denotes a lack of knowledge of history, unacceptable manipulation and despair of those who no longer have any argument but lies to support their political positions. [[Applauds]] As President of the Government I will say that all these statements have one answer: the way to solve our problems is the constitutional way, the way of the Spanish Constitution, the way of dialogue within the legality that we have given ourselves. Everything that is outside the Constitution and outside the Statute of Autonomy of Catalonia will have the answer - and I will say it in a very calm and paused way, so that I can understand it clearly - firm but serene, proportional and forceful of the social and democratic rule of law. [[Applauds]] There's no need to say more. You don't have to gesticulate or speak louder to make me understand. My commitment and my determination are reflected in the Journal of Sessions. [[Rumors]] Ladies and gentlemen, the reason for my position is based on the same conviction, and I defend plural Spain, a Spain proud of its territorial diversity. I defend the Spanish Constitution and also its title VIII. I believe in the self-government of our autonomy, defending its improvement and reinforcement. Ladies and gentlemen, centralism is a historical error typical of the right which today, as yesterday, by opportunism again fall into the same. But that is not the history of the Spanish left or the history of Spanish liberalism. Autonomous Spain has brought democracy and progress to territories condemned for centuries to ostracism. Therefore, this government will not take a step back from the re-centralizing impulses of the Spanish right, nor will we accept - and I want this to be clear - a new violation of our constitutional and statutory order. Ladies and gentlemen, self-government is coexistence and this is what we must preserve all those responsible for politics. I have said this once, twice, three times and I will not tire of repeating it: the problem in Catalonia is not independence, the problem in Catalonia is coexistence. Ladies and gentlemen, in my first appearance before you in this House as President of the Government, I defined my Government as a feminist, progressive, ecological and, above all, Europeanist government. And I did so because I am aware that it is in the European arena and in the international defence of a multilateral order that is being called into question where Spain plays its present and its future to a large extent. The interdependence of our societies obliges us to do so. It is a vision opposed to that of those who defend projects of segregation and isolation; projects not only contrary to the logic of the times, but also to common sense. Both the Brexit and the Catalan independence movement walk in parallel and with similar rhetoric. In both cases there is a report of ailments invented and magnified by manipulation. In both cases it is intended to force the population to make binary decisions: it is obliged to choose between being European or British, between being Spanish or being Catalan, when we have lived with those identities and many others over decades. In both cases, a third party is always blamed, as long as one's own responsibilities in the cuts in education and health that erode social cohesion and trust in the institutions are removed. In both cases, the discordant is demonized and the population is forced into military action in the face of conflict. In both cases, a dream is built that, in colliding with reality, generates a huge flow of frustration that no one wants to manage - today the British Prime Minister is going to make a motion of censure from six to eight in the British Parliament - and those who do so or flee ahead or flee their responsibilities. Ladies and gentlemen, on 25 November the European Council endorsed - as is technically said in the diplomatic jargon - the withdrawal agreement and approved the political declaration establishing the framework for future relations between the European Union and the United Kingdom. It was the culmination of almost a year and a half of very complex negotiations stemming from the decision of the United Kingdom in June 2016 to opt out of the European Union; a decision of a country that we respect - of course - but which must make us reflect on the irresponsibility of a ruler, of Prime Minister Cameron, who turned a party problem, of the Tory Party, into a European and global problem. A decision that must also make us reflect on the dangers faced by democracy itself when divisive and confrontational elements come into play with an end, which is the political and social fracture of the country, and always with the same pattern, the primacy of visceral emotions over reason. A scenario in which language and feelings are manipulated to create a narrative contrary to the logic of the times. We've seen it in Brexit and we're seeing it in Catalonia. I want you to bear that in mind, ladies and gentlemen, with Brexit we are facing a movement that is moving against history and also against reason. The reason, what does it dictate to us? It dictates that global challenges demand global responses, require a common project, more integration, more unity, always recognizing the identity diversity of each, but also more fraternity, understanding to unite rather than divide, not to see complexity and plurality as a problem but as a source of opportunities and wealth of societies. Today some want to reduce this enriching complexity to a simple choice between exclusionary choices, forcing the choice between evil and greater evil, narrowing the public space to a choice between alternatives defined by what they oppose and never by what is proposed. They take advantage of the open gaps in inequality and frustration of an economic crisis and the wrong response of neo-conservative policies, which socialized losses and privatized profits. Here too, there is a common pattern: the same ones who pulled the cuts and austerity with conservative policies accuse a third party as an excuse to encourage separation. Brechas, in short, ladies and gentlemen, to invent an ill-treatment, an ill-treatment magnified with a single purpose, which is to polarize, to confront parts of society. We have seen it with Brexit in the United Kingdom, ladies and gentlemen, but also here we have witnessed that debate, fuelled by a succession of fallacies, false illusions and dialectic traps at the service of the same objective: to deny space for moderation and for understanding and to sign the most radical options. The worse, the better, ladies and gentlemen, could be summed up. Brexit will in any case have very painful consequences, whatever its outcome for a country with which Spain maintains close ties. More than 300 Spanish companies operate in that country, 25% of the total number of tourists we receive in our country every year are British, in the United Kingdom they study our sons and daughters - many of them - and Spain is the country of residence of the largest British citizen community on the continent. Look, ladies and gentlemen, two weeks ago the Bank of England released a demolishing report on the consequences of the Brexit. It warns that the withdrawal of the European Union has already had consequences for the British economy, reducing productivity growth, depreciating the pound, increasing inflation and, consequently, lowering real wage growth for workers. This is the present, this is the now, the predictions of the future, from the multiple still open scenarios, are even more dramatic, more demolishing. Taking as a reference May 2016 - the time before the referendum - the forecasts fluctuate in a decline of the gross domestic product from 1,25 % to 8,25 %, depending on a scenario of close cooperation - i.e. with an orderly exit from the United Kingdom of the European Union - or with the application of the rules of the World Trade Organisation - and consequently without agreement and in a disorderly way -. The translation of these numbers - the Bank of England tells us - in terms of unemployment and inflation in fact imply talking about an even greater economic recession than the one suffered in 2008 in the case of a disorderly and unagreed exit. I believe that with these figures published by the Bank of England it becomes even clearer and more real those warnings that were also given to Catalan independenceists by public and private bodies alerting them to the economic consequences that the independence of Catalonia from Spain and the European Union could have: a fall in the gross domestic product in Catalonia from 3.52% to 17.6%, a destruction of jobs that could exceed the rate of unemployment in Catalonia to 34% and an impact on the inability to provide financial resources to public services and the bankruptcy of social security and thus of pensioners. Let's go back to the UK. Ladies and gentlemen, in view of these figures published by the Bank of England, what can be said to British companies when uncertainty prevents them from making growth plans? What do you say to workers who are going to suffer a real - uninvented, real - loss of their purchasing power by increasing prices and cost of living? What can be said to young people in a country who have grown up, ladies and gentlemen, without knowing what a border was when they now have to hear again about concepts such as customs and visas? But it doesn't matter, the UK is sick of the experts, it held the leader of the Brexit campaign, Michael Gove, as if figures and facts no longer matter, as if the repetition of a falsehood a million times were to turn it into a reality. We are talking about the impoverishment of millions and millions of people, a real impact on their real lives and not a mere statistic. Ladies and gentlemen, it must be remembered that Brexit is starting out on a grotesque campaign of unprecedented lies and misinformation. For example, you will remember lies, ladies and gentlemen, like the 350 million pounds a week that the United Kingdom was going to receive back to finance - they said - its public health system with the exit from the European Union. Today the British, two years later, know that the problem was not in Brussels, it was never in Brussels, but in the cuts imposed by the British Conservative governments for years. By the way, others also invented fiscal balances and aggravations to justify EUR 16 billion of imaginary expoliation. Lies also like those that illustrated the image - I don't know if they remember - of a Syrian refugee caravan in another country that wasn't the United Kingdom, a denigrating image used as a background of lies with the far rightist Nigel Farage in the foreground, where xenophobic messages were being launched. Lies like Turkey's alleged imminent entry into the European Union in order to mobilize Islamophobic sentiments. Lies, ladies and gentlemen, such as the supposed instant immediacy with which a trade agreement between the United Kingdom and the European Union was to be signed, which reduced the sudden impact of automatic exit. Lies such as the idea that permanence in the single market was compatible with limiting the free movement of citizens or, as they said, with taking control of borders. Lies, in short, that we have seen reproduced almost in their literality here in Spain when we are talking about Catalonia. Independence has followed the same pattern of deception and exaltation of emotions as its British travel companions, and as a result has managed to crisscross and divide Catalan society as well as Spanish society. Lies such as the statement, for example, that the day after the suspension of the unilateral declaration of independence told us the independence leaders that initiatives of international mediation had been launched, do they remember? It was of no use that three days later the President of the European Commission made it clear that there was no possibility of talking about such a scenario. Lies like the one claimed that the path of independence - and I quote textually - would be undertaken to the law with seny - with common sense in Catalan. Difficult to square, ladies and gentlemen, that assertion with the flagrant bankruptcy of statutory legality in Catalonia in September 2017. From a false fiscal balance to a supposed ease with which they would enter Europe, through an allusion of business investment that would arrive, the discourse of the independence leaders has been to inflate the supposed advantages of that independence and to hide the suffering of Catalans; and if there were costs, they would pay others. And this is not a assumption, we have already seen how some have done so by circumventing their responsibilities, how it has happened, for example, also in the supporters of the Brexit, where one by one have resigned to avoid taking charge of managing the rupture and also the costs. Isaiah Berlin said that in politics there is nothing more perverse than supporting a few simple ideas with universal keys. [[Rumors]] That is the main lesson we can draw from Brexit, but also from each and every one of the processes that are built on lies to divide and to confront, with the complicity of unscrupulous means in spreading falsehoods, also, of course, in the Catalan independence process. Here and there we have witnessed the exaltation of a handful of topics covered with nostalgia by a past that can no longer return and always at the service of the rhetoric of us in front of them. Nothing better, by the way, than a referendum to achieve the goal of leaving society in two, a tool that some wanted to import by listening to, with cheating analogies, ignoring, for example, that the Scottish referendum, to which Catalan independence so much refers, is born from an international treaty more than three centuries ago between Scotland and the United Kingdom, or keeping alive the expectation of repetition, if the result is not satisfactory, as many times as it is necessary to repeat that referendum. It is happening in Scotland, where a second referendum is already being called for, it is also happening in the United Kingdom, where a second referendum is also being held in relation to those who lost that vote. And the consequences of that whole process are well known, we are seeing them now live and live: economic uncertainty and instability; frustration and social division; and, in the United Kingdom, an unprecedented constitutional crisis. Far from cohering the country, it has divided it for many years. The seams of the United Kingdom have jumped through Northern Ireland, where the Good Friday agreements are being jeopardized; through Scotland, without divining lasting solutions; and the division, in my opinion, greater and more serious, the division between generations, ladies and gentlemen, with hundreds of thousands of young people marching in favor of the remnant frustrated by seeing how the political irresponsibility of their rulers further increases the risks of an already uncertain future for those British young people. Ladies and gentlemen, the strength of a democracy is not born out of the position of absolute domination by a majority minority over the rest of the options. The strength of a democracy lives in the capacity to integrate solutions, to reach consensus, to explore the territory of the agreement, because in that territory, in the territory of the agreement, lies the will of the vast majority of the population to reconcile in coexistence, in the face of those who need confrontation, take advantage of it or deceive about the consequences of their political adventures. As you know, the British Parliament postponed yesterday the vote on the withdrawal agreement of the extraordinary Council on 25 November. It is a decision that will not change the Spanish position or the position of the European Union. That is what the European Commission itself, the European Council itself, made known yesterday, whose assessments, of course, are ours. This agreement was and remains the only one possible, and there is no other negotiation, there is no renegotiation. It is therefore for the United Kingdom to determine what decision it is going to take, even more so in the light of the decision of the European Court of Justice of 10 December last, which recognises the right of the United Kingdom to withdraw, to revoke the proceedings initiated with the activation of Article 50. Whatever the scenario we are facing, what I can assure you of is that the Spanish Government has taken the necessary measures to ensure that, if the withdrawal agreement is not adopted, the impact on Spain and its citizens is as little as possible. Ladies and gentlemen, I would like to explain some of the elements of this agreement, which I believe are important to all of you. This agreement ensures an orderly exit from the UK that minimizes the impact of Brexit on citizens and economic actors. It has achieved virtually all the objectives at the start of the negotiations. The first of these, and most important for the Spanish Government, is the safeguarding of the rights of Community citizens in the United Kingdom, where we have more than 300,000 citizens, and vice versa. This, as I said, has been one of the main priorities of the Spanish Government. In order to guarantee these rights, the agreement takes precedence over the domestic law of both parties and also has a direct effect, i.e. it will be directly applicable by the European courts and by the British courts. In short, and I believe this is the basic political message, because there are many families that have their children in the UK living or working, European citizens and British citizens will be protected by this agreement. The free movement of goods marketed after the end of the transitional period is also guaranteed in respect of markets and services. In judicial and police cooperation, the agreement provides for twelve legal instruments, such as the Treaty on Mutual Assistance in Criminal Matters, the European Arrest and Delivery Order or the European Investigation Order, to continue to apply until the end of the transitional period. The United Kingdom will also continue to participate in existing joint research teams. Also, as you know, the agreement includes a transitional period until 31 December 2020, and this period will essentially allow the United Kingdom, but also the 27 Member States of the European Union, to adapt to the new reality, if it happens. During the same period the legislation and acquis communautaire will continue to apply to the United Kingdom although it is no longer part of the institutions. This is also an important issue of the agreement. The United Kingdom will also continue to meet its financial obligations as if it were a Member State. The manufacturing sector, the fishing sector, the exporters of fruit and vegetables, as well as the industrial sector of our country will also find in the withdrawal agreement an appropriate framework for continuing their export activity; it is also positive for our companies with greater investment activity, also for financial services, and of course for a vital sector for our economy as is the tourism sector. The United Kingdom will leave the internal market, but this agreement has the elements necessary for the intensity of our economic relationship to be maintained with rules of play, in my opinion, balanced and fair. In addition to the withdrawal agreement, the European Council on 25 November also approved the so-called political declaration, which sets out the future relationship between the European Union and the United Kingdom. This text clearly underlines the principles and red lines of the European Union, which are, firstly, respect for its autonomy to decide; secondly, the defence of integrity in our internal market; and thirdly, the defence of the four freedoms which define the European project: the movement of goods, services, capital, and above all, and above all, of people. Ladies and gentlemen, this is the withdrawal agreement and we are not going to negotiate another, as I said before. What has not been done in this year and a half of very complex negotiations will not be achieved in three months. The annex to the withdrawal agreement, the political declaration, sets out as its objective the search for an intense, balanced relationship in rights and obligations for the future, and this relationship will also include an area of free trade and sectoral cooperation. I would like to dwell, because it has been the subject of debate, on the question of Gibraltar. The priority, as you know, of the Government of Spain in relation to Gibraltar has always been the same: to provide peace and security for the people of the Campo de Gibraltar. This is an absolute priority to meet the challenges faced by the people of a region that is severely affected by their economic situation. You know that the Government has adopted a special development plan for the Gibraltar Field, and our will is to reach agreement in all areas to enable the economic development of the area, in terms of employment and also in terms of welfare. This debate, ladies and gentlemen, does not affect the position that Spain has always maintained in relation to Gibraltar's sovereignty, but we cannot fall into the temptation to mortgage the present or project uncertainty on the daily lives of thousands of people living and working in the area. The claim of full sovereignty over Gibraltar is perfectly compatible with the search for agreements of all kinds, economic, social, financial, fiscal or military, within the framework of the Atlantic Alliance to prevent the suffering of the population from the consequences of a non-agreement. I would therefore like to convey, first of all, a message of confidence and certainty to the people of the area; they are the absolute priority of this Government. Our country has achieved by far the objectives that were proposed at the beginning of the negotiations. There it is collected in the British press, and this has also been recognised by European leaders. I will simply quote the words of the German Foreign Minister who said textually: 'The Gibraltar document is a legally binding document, so for the German Government this issue is settled. I do not know in the European Union - the German minister said - anyone who has another interpretation It is a binding agreement - the foreign minister continues - and everyone will respect it.' [[you see that you have not heard the opposition of this country]] Similarly, the President of the European Parliament, the President of the European Commission, the President of the European Council, and yesterday the Director of the European Commission's Madrid office, and we hope that the opposition in this matter will recognise the correctness of the Spanish Government, even if for once. [[Applauds]] Ladies and gentlemen, we have negotiated a bilateral protocol that will apply only until the end of this period, and from then on it will govern for Gibraltar what has been negotiated on the future relationship, and it, ladies and gentlemen, will depend on Spain's consent. Let it be clear: Article 184 does not include Gibraltar in the territorial sphere of the future relationship. Furthermore - and this is very important - we have obtained a political statement from the European Council and the exclusive Commission on the territorial scope of negotiations on the future relationship. Firstly, Spain's prior agreement is required for the negotiation of any European Union agreement that applies to Gibraltar. Secondly, Gibraltar is excluded from the general negotiations between the European Union and the United Kingdom. Agreements affecting Gibraltar - if any - will be the subject of separate agreements and will have to count - as has already been said - with the consent of Spain. Spain's negotiating position is therefore substantially strengthened with regard to past times. Finally, I would like to highlight a very relevant fact in my view, given the context and the current situation. In the event that the United Kingdom does not ratify the withdrawal agreement, the declaration of the European Council and also of the European Commission will remain in force, it will not lapse. Yes, the withdrawal agreement or the political declaration does, but not the agreement we have reached on Gibraltar and the support of the European Commission and the European Council. We have therefore defined the framework for the European Union's relations with Gibraltar for the coming decades, and be sure, ladies and gentlemen, that these relations pass through Spain. That is the reality, however much some want to feed with false news and exaggerations a debate that should be beyond party interests, as we did when we arrived at the Government. Ladies and gentlemen, as I said to you, last Monday we heard the British Parliament's decision to suspend the vote on the withdrawal agreement, and I would like to send a message of calm from this rostrum, as I did at the last appearance on the European Council. The Government has worked in all possible scenarios of Brexit regardless of the terms on which such an orderly or disorderly withdrawal takes place. We will continue to work closely with the European Commission - as we are doing -, with the European Council - as we are doing - and with the other Member States to protect our interests in the event of an unagreed exit. On the other hand, the Government has worked at the national level to identify the potential effects of an unagreed Brexit in all areas, in order to articulate contingency plans to safeguard our interests. Finally, I would like to reiterate the appeal I made to this House last October that companies operating in the United Kingdom should develop contingency and adaptation plans to reduce the impact of the UK's exit, whatever the scenario that finally takes place from 29 March next year. Ladies and gentlemen, I have detailed the essential content of an agreement that has been drawn up, as I have said before, over months of hard work; an agreement on which new questions are now being raised, in the light of the British Government's decision not to put to the vote the terms of the document, which I stress is not going to change its tenor as a result of the British Parliament's change of position with regard to the British Government, and I would like to draw your attention to an additional lesson that we must all draw from this scenario: the immense flow of time, effort and resources that we have lost along the way with Brexit; time, effort and resources that are very necessary to move forward in the design of the future for the European Union, to close the social and confidence gaps opened up by the recession, to discuss a path of regeneration and union around a project called Europe, and with which Spain is going to be today and always committed. We have an opportunity to show this commitment from tomorrow at the European Council to be held in Brussels, where we will deal with matters very relevant to the future of the European Union, firstly - and this is fundamental - because we are talking about the CAP, we are talking about cohesion policies, the multiannual financial framework, the financial perspective for the period 2021-2027. I would like to tell you that the position of the Government of Spain is that this multiannual financial framework should be approved before the European elections in May 2019. And today I would also like to reiterate before this House the commitment of the Government of Spain to adequate and sufficient funding in the next financial period. Secondly, there will also be discussions on how to complete and adapt the single market to the new digital economy, taking into account other policies such as industry, the environment and also innovation. We are also going to address issues relating to migration policy, and at this point, ladies and gentlemen, I would like to reaffirm the commitment of the Spanish Government to stable support from the European Union for Morocco in the medium and long term. It is a real priority, as you know - I have said it many times - of first order for our country in order to be able to successfully address migration policy in our country and also in the neighbouring country. An important issue is that the European Council is also going to talk about climate change. You know that the COP24 meeting took place on 3 December in the Polish city of Katowice, and the Council should be involved in the proposal for a long-term climate strategy with a view to reaching an agreement already in the first half of next year. After the Council, the euro summit will take place: we will discuss the reform proposals for the eurozone in which Spain is going to shift a conviction, and it is that these agreed measures that we will support over the next two days go in the right direction, but they are insufficient for the Spanish Government. Greater ambition is needed, we need to strengthen our institutional architecture in the face of future crises, and above all in something fundamental to this Government, which is the social sphere. A monetary area that does not have a stabilisation instrument will always remain unfinished. This is what we have advocated in all the Councils, so we must move decisively towards a common budget that promotes convergence, competitiveness; we need, ultimately, tools to ensure that the level of investment is maintained during the economic crises, and move forward - this is one of the proposals we are leading in the European Council - in a common unemployment insurance that complements current national systems. It is ultimately a question of strengthening the European project politically, of combating public disaffection, relegitimizing the mechanisms of solidarity: to greater real solidarity, ladies and gentlemen, less space for hate speech; to greater social Europe, less space for rhetoric contrary to European values. This is the position that the Government will stand for at the European Council, which must serve, among other things, to look to the future, to devote all our energies, all the efforts to think about the Europe of the next decade, a project in which Spain will always have the leading vocation to unite and to move forward, never to divide or to retreat in a sense contrary to the logic of history. Ladies and gentlemen, I have alluded throughout my presentation to two ideas with which I want to frame the political regeneration project that this government is pushing, and it is that of transformation and that of union. I would like to keep both ideas in mind when dealing with the political situation in Catalonia at this point in my speech. I start from a previous conviction: I am fully convinced that the majority of Catalan society rejects unilateralism and longs for encounter and coexistence. This is what all the opinion studies say, which place the majority of citizens on the path of dialogue and the improvement of self-government; but even more so: not only do the opinion studies say this, it is dictated by the wisdom, the space of moderation in which the majority of Catalan society and Spanish society are located, which demand solutions from politics and not the enconation of positions, and in that space, ladies and gentlemen, they will find us, they will find us, the Government of Spain, in that of moderation and in that of sensible Spain that in Catalonia wants to regain common sense. As I have already said, the Executive defends the self-government throughout Spain that enshrines our Constitution, Title VIII, and it does so against those who point to a re-centralizing rhetoric that reproduces the mistakes of the past and leads only to failure. With the Brexit we could see to what extent - ladies and gentlemen, allow me this digression - marginal political forces could condition the discourse of an entire party like the Tory Party, today plunged into disconcertingness. At the beginning of my speech I told them that the situation in Catalonia requires the strengthening of our common project, in Spain. I believe that in order to combat independence, to combat these impulses of territorial fragmentation, what we have to do is to articulate a common project, a project of regeneration in our country, a plural Spain that is recognized in its diversity, which also defends equality between citizens, and consequently to strengthen that common project is to launch a project, first of all, of moral and political regeneration, of modernization of our economy and of strengthening social cohesion. First, the moral regeneration and the consequent demand for public exemplaryity came from the hand of a motion of censure that brought out the noble task of the political thing from the mud of corruption. [[Rumors]] Secondly, a political regeneration that continued with the lifting of the veto imposed on the House by the previous parliamentary majority of the People's Party and Citizens' Party. As a result, there are strong social projects in the field of rights, freedoms and labour dignity under parliamentary procedure, along with the implementation of the resolutions of the House, such as the exhumation of the dictator and the constitutional reforms to limit the strangulations and the new definition of article 49, which is a fair demand of the group of persons with disabilities. [[Applauds]] Thirdly, the modernization of our economy, a project that goes so far as to reverse harmful elements of labour reform, and in this regard I would like to ask those parliamentary groups that are dealing with it that Article 42 of the Workers' Statute be reformed in Congress as soon as possible; to approve a youth employment plan that will mobilise EUR 2 billion over the next three years, to reduce the youth unemployment rate by 10 points; to promote the Dignity Employment Master Plan, which has brought out thousands and thousands of self-employed fakes from labour exploitation; the transposition of directives abandoned by the previous Government; the creation of the macro-prudential authority and the institution defending the financial client; the impetus to the industry with the activation of industrial agendas in multiple sectors; the approval of the artist's status; the just energy transition with the people and with the damaged territories; a modernization, ladies and gentlemen, which also passes through the fight against and adaptation to climate change with the law that we will adopt before the end of this year. I would also say to the parliamentary groups that equal work between men and women should be dealt with more quickly by this House, by our commitment to territorial cohesion and infrastructure, by the defence in Brussels of our agricultural, livestock and fisheries sector, by the commitment to the CAP and the cohesion funds, by the commitment decided by the science and research of this government as multipliers in the productivity of our economy. Many of these measures are already under way, ladies and gentlemen; others are part of the budgetary alternative that the Government will present next January and the agenda for change, which we will also present in the coming months, with the firm commitment of the Government of Spain to consolidate our public accounts fiscally. I told them about moral and political regeneration, I told them about modernising our economy, all of which will only be possible from social cohesion. Spain can and must grow by better redistributing the fruits of that growth. That's why we're going to approve the rise in the interprofessional minimum wage, that's why we've approved the extension of the social energy bonus. That is why we have regained the universality of public health and reversed educational cuts or also lost rights, such as the temporary disability allowance for public employees. That is why we have eliminated unjustifiable discrimination for groups with diabetes, HIV or celiac disease for access to public employment. That is why we have regained the right of women alone to insemination in the public health service portfolio. It is social cohesion that shields us from polarizing tensions, and it is social cohesion that also leads us to shield the upgrading of pensions to the CPI or to want to adopt a law to combat the violence suffered by minors. That is why we have also resolutely and resourcefully promoted the State Pact against Gender Violence. Ladies and gentlemen, at this point I want to be very frank and very determined, especially for those political forces that are negotiating with the far right. The path of equality between men and women is irreversible, as is clear from the nostalgics of a time on which this country has moved. [[Applauds]] There are those who widen or reduce their ideological margins depending on the context and the negotiation. In our case I say this loud and clear: we are going to continue to advance women's rights, we are going to defend those who suffer some kind of exclusion and we are also going to fight against the re-centralizing threats that are being defended by the People's and Citizens' Party. [[Applauds]] The Government governs, looks to the future and faces the present with a firm step, from a project of political and moral regeneration, of modernization of our economy and of strengthening social cohesion. We are laying the foundations for the great transformation that Spain needs to win a decade in this changing era that we live in. That is the philosophy of the draft budgets that we are going to present at the beginning of 2019; a project that stands on five axes. One, education, science and culture; two, strengthening our welfare state and thus opening up a debate on taxation in our country; three, the labour market and the necessary entrepreneurial drive; four, the environment and the energy transition; and five, the regeneration of our institutions with territorial cohesion as a fundamental pillar. That is the road map of a Government that advances and appeals to wisdom to overcome challenges that, above all, require conviction, common sense and responsibility on the part of all. This is a shared task, which depends not only on the Government, mainly on the Government, but also on the parliamentary groups present here and on the autonomous communities. As far as the territorial debate is concerned, ladies and gentlemen, as far as Catalonia is concerned, I would like to tell you that the government is based on two certainties. First - I said it before -, what is at stake is coexistence and not independence. Independence as such is a project in my opinion that goes against history, that is outside the European project and that does not count - I stress, does not count - with the social majority of Catalans and Catalans. Secondly, the solution - I said it from the first minute - requires time, dialogue and loyalty; dialogue first between Catalans. The Catalan parliamentary forces must forge a new agreement within the constitutional framework, representing those three fifths of the parliamentary groups that are needed in the Catalonian Parliament to reform the Catalonian Autonomy Statute. That you do not have in the Parliament of Catalonia. So the first thing independence forces have to do is recognize the non-nationalist side of Catalonia and start forging a great deal of agreement between Catalans, no matter whether they are nationalists or non-nationalists. This is something you have to do. [[Applauds]] Therefore dialogue first between Catalans. And loyalty, ladies and gentlemen, loyalty; loyalty of the opposition with the Government of Spain. Ladies and gentlemen, the shortest line between two points will always be the agreement and with that premise we have approached the establishment of bilateral commissions from the first minute between the Government of Spain and the Generalitat de Catalunya, which have been inactive since 2011. We have reduced litigation before the Constitutional Court; we have encouraged the return of Catalonia to forums such as the Technical Financing Commission to talk about a new model of autonomous financing; we have reached agreements on financing public services, pending investments, strategic infrastructures for the coming years in just six months; agreements with real impact on the real economy of Catalonia, such as the Mediterranean corridor, the La Sagrera station or the rolling plan. This is the path that the Spanish Government wants to continue to take in relation to Catalonia, ladies and gentlemen; this path has continuity in the adoption of budgets that represent a project of modernization and future for our society. A project to tackle the great transformations that our economy needs, to regain social and territorial cohesion after years of blockade, desidiy and paralysis. I do not want to quantify some of the measures that include these budgets in abstract terms; I want to do so in much closer terms, appealing to people rather than to territories, especially in this case appealing to Catalan society, because, look, the revaluation of pensions will benefit one and a half million Catalan pensioners. The increase in minimum and non-contributory pensions will benefit more than 300 000 Catalans and Catalans. Recovering the unemployment benefit for over fifty-two years of age will benefit more than 20,000 Catalans and Catalans. Paternity permits extended from five to eight weeks will benefit more than 36,000 Catalans and Catalans. This, ladies and gentlemen, is the path of dialogue, of the coexistence that the Government of Spain wants to go through. The one that allows us to find ourselves in a project to build, to transform Spain and with it also modernize Catalonia. Consequently, rejecting this project is impossible to close the social gap that still remains to be felt in much of Catalan society and Spanish society. Rejecting dialogue means relegating citizens, denying the reality of improving the quality of life in return for pursuing a chimera that will end - I guarantee - in a new frustration. Ladies and gentlemen, all I ask is for us to look outside, learn from the mistakes of other societies, draw lessons and act accordingly. Politicians are not at the service of the extremes, but of the vast majority of citizens who sit in the coordinates of moderation, security and social welfare. Brexit offers lessons that should be learned to confront those who make division their only alternative, and the most important lesson of all is the need to strengthen our democracy in the face of lies, alternative facts and the denial of reality and its real consequences, unfortunately very real, on people. It is precisely one of the most relevant aspects to be dealt with in this Council tomorrow that is related to the threat posed by the mechanisms of misinformation to our democracy, something which has been very present in the Brexit. If we humanize loss, if we are able to see the human dimension of division and fracture it is easier for us to find ourselves in the primacy of reason in the face of viscerality. In the case of Brexit, as in the case of those who advocate an internal tear in Catalonia, there is no winner, all are losers, especially the most humble, and also and especially the young. This reflection should be taken into account. The first duty of a ruler is to care for and guarantee social peace, coexistence. No democratic political project deserves to put at risk nothing or anyone, much less the lives of others. Ladies and gentlemen, in all societies there are crises and conflicts, and to solve them there is only one possible way, which is the constitutional way, which we have taken in Spain, the way of the Statute of Autonomy of Catalonia, the way of coexistence and the recognition of plural identities built after centuries of living together. So there you will always find us, ladies and gentlemen, never on the path of hatred, confrontation and irresponsibility. We start from a common legacy that I want to appeal to once again. He warned the President of the Republic, Manuel Azaña, that it is the responsibility of politics not to condemn every generation to invent fire, to burn their hands; to maintain wisdom in the end, to avoid the temptation to believe that history begins in itself, and that everything that precedes us has been and is a mistake. The European Union is a success in its history, in a history of more than sixty years, a history that will transcend the disagreements in the management of a dramatic moment such as the economic crisis, and whose consequences, such as Brexit, we are still paying for. In the same way the collective project of a plural Spain that adds identities, from a unique diversity that enriches us, will overcome, I have no doubt, this hour with the teachings of a shared journey. A path that no one has the right to erase from Adamism to believe that history begins with us here and now. Thank you very much, Madam President; thank you very much, ladies and gentlemen. ParlaMint-ES_2018-12-12-CD181212.u32	Ladies and gentlemen, Members of Parliament, President, ladies and gentlemen, no one can deny that this year has been moved, like a roller coaster: now up, straight down and back to the beginning. And like such a roller coaster, there are no lack of emotions and curves, especially many curves, with surprises to each elbow. Few will deny that the roller coaster is the biggest attraction you can find in a theme park. Whether you like it or not, you always run the risk of ending up there. For many it has a fatal attraction: riding on a soft roller coaster, provides adrenaline, emotions and, even if you imagine it, you never know what the fall behind the slope is like or whether the speed in the spinner curves will make you lose your hat and win a few reeds. There are many types of people who end up lining up on the roller coaster. There are those that go brave and put themselves in the first row of the first wagon, on many occasions to be able to face the rest that they are more leaders than anyone else, that the park is safe, that the roller coaster is theirs and that all those who do not point to the first row are loose, because, what gives more how fast you go, the wagons supposedly will never derail. And indeed, there is always the companion who ends up sitting in the front row and shouting 'we all want more' so that the rest do not doubt him. I also believe that these are old debates which must be underlined and recovered in order to know where we are, Mr Campuzano. Mr Juncker - and so many other members and leaders of the European Union - said the following textually about the eventual independence of a particular territory of a Member State: If a territory of a Member State ceases to be a part of that State, because that territory becomes an independent State, the treaties cannot continue to apply to that part of the territory. - Point. And the new independent region becomes a third country as a result of its independence. From the very origins of man, population groups have moved from one place to another in search of more food, better land or a more benign climate. Migration movements have often also occurred as a result of persecution and war. Today, the first cause of immigration to Europe is the permanent plundering of natural resources, which leave countries originating in immigrants by European capitals in misery and war. Paradoxically, natural resources travel on five-star ships, while immigrants, their original owners, have to travel in sad pateras. Migration flows in today's world are very intense and respond to two fundamental causes, economic or political, and are directed towards countries in need of labour. There are ILO forecasts that, until at least 2050, European countries will need immigrant labour. It was not by chance that United Nations member countries met a few days ago to try to manage migration flows in a comprehensive and international manner. Nor was it a coincidence that most of these countries, more than one hundred and fifty, had adopted a global pact for migration, the first such agreement, and I am pleased that Spain was one of them. Spain, instead of having a State policy against migration, has opted in recent years for the Aliens Act and, instead of favouring emigration as a State, makes the benefits to be borne by employers and large capital. The precarious work that this law achieves and that encourages the submerged economy leads even to the lower wages of other workers. It is clear that the availability of undocumented workers willing to work at almost any cost in order to survive and to obtain an employer who makes a contract of employment that facilitates the obtaining of a residence permit has a negative effect on the salary of some sectors of the population, especially those who are characterized by more manual work, in addition to having fewer social security contributors, even in order to be able to finance pensions and to ensure the increase that is permanently matched with the CPI. One hundred and twenty older than sixty-four years are counted for each hundred and fewer than sixteen years, according to the latest data from the National Institute of Statistics. As an example and for the benefit of all, I will refer to what would be a temporary modification of a precept of foreign legislation. Persons who are eligible for work authorization on an exceptional basis of social roots must have three years of continuous stay in the pattern, a one-year employment contract and a report proving that they are rooted. In Catalonia in the last three years, from 2015 to 2017, some 20,000 people, around 7000 per year, obtained work permits. We have calculated that in an exceptional and temporary process, where the stay of three to one year is changed and the requirement for a contract of employment of one year to three months is changed, there would be at least 20,000 more people in a position to be regularised, calculating downwards. ParlaMint-ES_2018-12-12-CD181212.u279	Thank you very much, Mrs. Surra. On behalf of the Government, I now give the floor to the Minister for Labour, Migration and Social Security.
ParlaMint-ES_2018-12-12-CD181212.u280	Thank you very much, President. Thank you very much for your intervention, Your Honor. Believe me, I share your interest in the integration of immigrants into our country; I share it and, of course, my entire Government, led by its President. This Government believes that it is not so much a question of integrating the irregular immigrant population but of ensuring that all those who come to our country do so by legal means and are fully integrated. I mean, we're betting on an orderly, secure, legal immigration. Your Honor, immigration is a phenomenon, as you have just said, inevitable and also necessary and positive. You have heard me say on more than one occasion - and you have also said it here this afternoon - that it is as ancient as the history of humanity. Unavoidable by the presence of various causes that you have also highlighted: war conflicts, environmental migration, an increasingly globalised society and, above all, by increasing inequality. In short, there is a runaway effect of misery, disease, political persecution and war confrontations. It is also a necessary phenomenon because the demographic evolution of our societies points this out. According to the report on population forecasts published by Airef last October, Spain needs the average arrival of 270 000 net annual immigrants by 2050. The International Monetary Fund has also warned our country that maintaining the Social Security System will require the arrival of more than 5 million immigrants by 2050. Of course we have a challenge, which is to raise the birth rate. The data you have also commented on are worrying, the lowest birth rate since there are records and a great aging of the population. Finally, it is a tremendously positive phenomenon for all the parties involved in it, of course for migrants and their countries of origin, but also for the countries of destination. The positive effect of immigration on the Spanish economy is more than contrasted. According to a report by the economic office of the Caixa, 30 per cent of GDP growth since the mid-1990s and the first decade of the 21st century was a result of the arrival of immigrants, an effect that climbed to 50 per cent between 2000 and 2005. That is why Spain ratified the United Nations-sponsored Global Compact on Secure, Regular and Orderly Migration last Monday in Marrakech because of its commitment to regular, secure and orderly immigration. This is a historic agreement that constitutes the first real effort to build the new migration regime of the twenty-first century, and our country, ladies and gentlemen, will be at the forefront of its defence and its implementation. We understand that it is now more than ever essential to establish a global governance of human mobility; the times that go by demand it given the rise of racist and xenophobic explosions that are trying to bring our society back to a climate of confrontation that is tremendously negative. And that commitment to legal immigration also leads my ministry to work on the development of pilot projects and the substantial improvement of existing projects. Among them, we will approve this month the order to regulate the collective management of contracts in origin for the year 2019. The order, in line with the objectives set out in the said global pact, allows the development of circular immigration processes, legal, orderly and secure immigration, beneficial to all parties. However, Your Honor, this government's vocation to promote legal and orderly immigration is not incompatible with the struggle we have to fight against irregular immigration that is harmful to all parties involved. It harms immigrants themselves, who are deprived of their rights, subjected to labour exploitation processes, used by human trafficking networks, relegated to social invisibility and to the status of second-class citizens. It also harms the countries of origin, which are deprived of remittances and of circular mobility to enable the productive flow of wealth and knowledge, and it also harms our country, because fewer taxes and contributions are paid, situations of unfair competition between companies are generated and social cohesion is undoubtedly difficult. Therefore, ladies and gentlemen, we cannot accept irregular immigration as a rule. We are obliged to put an end to this irregular immigration, because it runs counter to the assumptions that of course allow us to enter or remain legally in our country and that is also required by European legislation. However, on these sometimes painful expulsion processes, I would like to make it very clear that any expulsion, any return must be carried out in strict compliance with the legislation in force. This is individualized, with guarantee of legal assistance and, where appropriate, with an interpreter to care for the person concerned at that time. I would also like to make it very clear that every immigrant who resides among us, regardless of his or her administrative situation, holds a number of rights which this Government undertakes to respect scrupulously, as demanded by our Constitutional Court, among others in its judgment 236/2007 of 7 November, concerning migration matters. In this judgment, the High Court points out that foreigners are holders of all the rights which the Constitution does not expressly and exclusively reserve to Spanish nationals, as well as of any rights which may be granted to them on the basis of the Universal Declaration of Human Rights. These rights are therefore recognized and belong to individuals in respect of them, regardless of their nationality and administrative status. Among other things, and in a paradigmatic way, irregular immigrants enjoy the ownership of key rights in their integration process and also in the configuration of our welfare State, such as the right to education and the right to health. In this regard, I would like to remind you that one of the first commitments made by President Pedro Sánchez in his intervention in the motion of censure of 31 May, which was implemented last July, is the restoration of universal access to the National Health System broken by the Government of the People's Party by withdrawing the health card for irregular immigrants, in what constituted a radically unjust and unworthy measure, negative for immigrants and also for public health. It is one of the measures taken by this Government that I feel, believe me, personal and especially most proud of because it demonstrates a clear will to protect the rights of immigrants. In this regard let me turn to our saying: We must not only preach but also give wheat. Few measures, ladies and gentlemen, have a more direct impact on the proper integration of the immigrant population than on facilitating their access to public health. This Government is therefore going to facilitate the exercise of all constitutionally recognized rights to immigrants regardless of their administrative status, which does not mean that we are currently considering any process of regularization. It is true that in 2004 the biggest extraordinary process of regularization of immigrants registered in Spain was carried out with great success, but it must not be forgotten that it responded to a very different scenario from the current one, a scenario defined by the huge irregular immigration exchange left by the Government of the People's Party and by the lack of legal channels to regularize the situation of these people. Alongside this, we then saw a job market in good times, with minimum unemployment rates and many unfilled jobs, already abandoned by a higher qualification of Spanish workers. In view of this need, the social partners were also asking us for an answer. The situation is very different now. At present, which was not the case before, the law articulates sufficient avenues for persons in an irregular situation to apply under certain circumstances for a residence permit without the need to return to their country of origin. These four exceptional circumstances - you have commented on some of them - are: international protection, humanitarian reasons, collaboration with administrative, judicial, police and prosecutorial authorities, and, the most important of them, rooting for social, family and labour reasons; measures put in place, as you will know, by the Government of José Luis Rodríguez Zapatero through the regulations for the development of the Organic Law on the Rights and Freedoms of Foreigners in Spain and their social integration. Those who have entered our country irregularly will therefore be able to avail themselves of one of the exceptional mechanisms of regularization that I have just mentioned; if not, the corresponding return file will have to be initiated. In short, Your Honor, what is at stake is to guarantee universal rights to all people in our country and to work for a legal and fully integrated immigration. To achieve such full integration is a priority objective of the migration policy of this Government as it has been in all socialist Governments. Since 1994, they have adopted the first plan for the social integration of immigrants. There have been a few years of paralysis and neglect on the part of the People's Party Government in which this integration completely disappeared from the political discourse. In this regard, let me tell you what the two essential instruments of our integration policy are going to be. First of all, we are drawing up a new strategic plan for citizenship and integration - the President announced the other day - so that integration will once again be part of the public agenda, again involving both civil society and all public and private institutions that want to cooperate in it. Four will be the basic principles of this strategy: equality, citizenship, interculturality and social inclusion. On the basis of these principles, the Government will manage the diversity of the immigration phenomenon and introduce it into all its public policies: education, health, employment, housing, women, participation, coexistence, neighbourhood, children. As for the state of development of this strategy, the Secretariat of State for Migration is engaged in a process of consultations that will lead to a strategic guidance document on integration to be completed by 31 March next year. Secondly, because the citizenship plan without a fund that supports its policies would be a mere declaration of intent, we will reactivate the State Fund for the Integration of Immigrants, eliminated by the Government of the People's Party, in order to collaborate with the autonomous communities and municipalities in this integration goal. The commitment of this Government is to replenish this fund in the coming budgets and to do so sufficiently, so that it will also mobilize additional funds from these public administrations. These two instruments, from the Secretariat of State for Migration, are financed through calls for grants to non-profit social entities, programmes promoting the social and labour integration of immigrants, and from applicants for international protection covering various aspects: comprehensive reception projects for basic needs and support for the integration of immigrants; projects co-financed with the European Social Fund in the field of employment; and projects financed by the FAMI, the Asylum, Migration and Integration Fund. These are projects, among others, aimed at acquiring basic knowledge of the language, history, institutions, order, culture, framework of coexistence and democratic values in Spanish society, as well as those aimed at the integration of children and young people into the education system, those aimed at the promotion and prevention of health, the promotion of equal treatment and programmes specifically aimed at women. I conclude, ladies and gentlemen, by reaffirming the twofold commitment of the Government of Spain, on the one hand, to combating irregular immigration, which benefits no one, and, on the other, to promoting the development of an orderly, legal and fully integrated immigration. Our country, the Spanish citizenship and the immigrants themselves will win by it. It is also a moral obligation and a question of justice, to realize a right to protection and prosperity beyond our passport. Thank you very much. [[Applauds]]

ParlaMint-ES_2018-12-12-CD181212.u285	Thank you very much, Minister. - the Basque parliamentary group on the phenomenon of torture and ill-treatment in the Spanish state. [[EAJ-PNV]] [[File number 172/000168]]
ParlaMint-ES_2018-12-12-CD181212.u286	We turn to the urgent inquiry by the Basque Parliamentary Group into the phenomenon of torture and ill-treatment in the Spanish State. [[EAJ-PNV]] For your defense, Mr. Legarda Uriarte has the floor.
ParlaMint-ES_2018-12-12-CD181212.u287	Thank you very much, President. On 12 October 2008, during the IX Legislature and coinciding with the 60th Anniversary of the Universal Declaration of Human Rights, the Council of Ministers approved the elaboration of a human rights plan in compliance with the final provision of the 1993 Vienna Conference, which was presented by the Vice-President of the Government to the United Nations Assembly, stating that it was an ambitious plan that would put Spain at the forefront of the world and serve the Government as a framework for its actions for the promotion and protection of human rights. Beyond the catalogue of fundamental rights that the Constitution collects and those that it welcomes through the reception of international agreements by making them part of our system, it can be said that if human rights cease to be promoted, they begin to weaken. That is why it is a commitment that must be renewed on a daily basis by all the institutions in all their actions and decisions. Now, having just completed the 70th Anniversary of the Universal Declaration of Human Rights and announced by the Government the elaboration of a second human rights plan, the Basque Parliamentary Group considers that the time has come to put in place concrete measures for the recognition and reparation of victims of human rights violations for having suffered torture, cruel, inhuman or degrading treatment in the democratic period and in the context of the fight against the unjustifiable terrorism of ETA. These victims have waited too long, the institutions have been delayed in their recognition and reparation, except we consider - from the Basques and, despite this, have only been the subject of misunderstanding and unbiased criticism. The Basque Parliamentary Group, as I said, considers this to be a moment of truth and justice. It is about opening the path to truth, justice and reparation, and also opening a path to peace, reconciliation and coexistence. This path, in the field of state institutions, began a path of general recognition with Law 52/2007, called the historical memory, as an expression of the right of all citizens to moral reparation, recognizing and declaring the radically unjust nature of any form of personal violence produced for political, ideological or belief reasons during the Civil War and those suffered by the same causes during the Francoist dictatorship and repression. For this reason, the recognition and reparation of violations of rights and abuses by or with the connivance and tolerance of public authorities is peaceful or less conflictive when it applies to the pre-constitutional period, in which it is well known that the repressive methods of any non-democratic regime have been used and do not enjoy the presumption of legitimacy that is presumptuous to the actions carried out in the current rule of law. However, this same recognition and reparation is more problematic when it comes to the constitutional period, and perhaps its explanation is that these are situations which, to some extent, escape the normality of a democratic regime that was not subject to the tension of terrorist violence. That is why this situation is particularly sensitive because of the interests faced: on the one hand, legal certainty, the exclusivity of the jurisdictional function or the prestige or good name of the vast majority of public officials of the State Security Forces and Bodies and, on the other hand, the reality of certain abuses and the right of the real victims to recognition and reparation for the unjust damage suffered. However, with regard to these abuses during the constitutional period, the State institutions have had some timid reaction, as was the setting up of a special commission of inquiry into alleged ill-treatment of detainees in the Basque Country, which was subsequently called a commission of inquiry into human rights, at the request of the Socialist, Communist parliamentary groups and the deputy of Euskadiko Ezkerra, Mr Bandrés, and which carried out its work between 1979 and 1982 in the Second Legislature. Although, finally, the Commission did not reach any conclusions, fourteen sessions were held, numerous visits were made to prisons and centres under the Ministry of the Interior, information was collected, hearings were held by political officials, public officials of the State Administration, the judiciary and the prosecutor's office, and dean of the Bar Associations of the Basque Country, and testimony was also collected from alleged victims of ill-treatment and their relatives. I would now like to point out, for the sake of example, that for the present day this Committee of Inquiry has to be set up and to carry out its work under infinitely worse conditions than we have today, when ETA has already ceased to exist. On those days of the Commission's activity, the chilling figure of 252 people was killed by ETA. But since those years already distant, initials of the course of the current constitutional period, a path, a path to the truth, justice and reparation of these victims of human rights violations caused by actions of unlawful repression in the context of the fight against ETA terrorism has not been regained from the institutions of the State; a path that, on the contrary, is being used by Basque institutions. I would like to recall the Basque Government's Decree 107/2012, chaired by the then Lehendakari Patxi López, which was supported by the People's Party, on the declaration and reparation of victims of human rights violations, or also the agreement of the Basque Parliament of June 2015, which, with the favourable votes of the Basque Nationalist Party, the Socialist Party, the People's Party and Bildu, urged the Basque Government to continue the policies of reparation for these victims and to agree on a law of reparation for these victims already in force. Because, ladies and gentlemen, these victims exist, they are not a fiction; they have been objectively recognized in reports from international human rights agencies, already since 1984 by Amnesty International and Human Rights Watch, and since the 1990s by the Committee on the Prevention of Torture and the United Nations Special Rapporteur against Torture; reports that have been repeated in successive visits and that have not been answered by the Spanish authorities in all these years. These reports have reiterated and coincidentally expressed their concern that the phenomenon of ill-treatment in Spain could not be considered outdated or that its existence could not be reduced to a mere random or sporadic case. Although we also have to say in honour of the truth - and we are deeply pleased - that since 2015 there has been no recorded case, coinciding precisely with the fact that no incommunicado police arrests were ordered, because it is found that the largest number of cases of torture and ill-treatment coincides with the largest number of incommunicado arrests, precisely in the 1980s and beginning in 2000. Such abusive treatment has also been found not only in the reports of international human rights organizations, but also in countless chronicles and information and in reports prepared by academic and expert bodies. I would like to recall here the basic report of human rights violations in the Basque case between 1960 and 2013, prepared, among others, by the current mayor of Madrid, Mrs Manuela Carmela, and Bishop Juan María Uriarte. I would also like to recall the report presented a year ago on torture and ill-treatment in the Basque Country between 1960 and 2014, which is the largest archive on cases of ill-treatment for the investigation of human rights violations, the work of future institutional mechanisms of recognition and for the collective memory of Euskadi. It is an effective investigation in line with international standards and the Istanbul Protocol of 2000, in the face of the inactivity of State institutions that have systematically denied the existence of such practices, which has been merely a further indicator, perhaps, of a lack of willingness to carry out an effective eradication or, if necessary, to conceal unjust sufferings that - I must also say - a very small minority of public servants could have caused and that come to question the other large majority that have never committed them. In any case, what really matters is to look only at the victims, think exclusively of them to make their recognition possible, because terrorist actions did not justify a single violation by abuse of power, but the existence of human rights violations cannot be presented as a confrontation between violence caused by political conflict either. Under no circumstances can it be intended, even in a veiled manner, to contextualize ETA terrorism, to equate violence or victims, to justify terrorist violence under the assumption of police abuse, or to erode the social and democratic rule of law, but to strengthen it, without prejudice to the fact that this issue of abuse of power must be dealt with, in fact, with prudence, consensus and sensitivity. Nor can it serve to erode coexistence, but, on the contrary, to normalize and build it from the memory and reparation of any human rights violation. Minister, we believe that what should prevail - and I am finalizing, Madam President - is support for victims, so that the damage is recognized, and this regardless of whether the victim is identified, apprehended, tried or convicted, as mandated by UN Resolution 60/47, the European Convention on the Compensation of Victims of Violent Crimes, Directive 2012/29, already transposed by Law 4/2015, of the Victims' Statute, or even, as a comparison parameter, the same Law 29/2011, on the comprehensive protection of victims of terrorism, in which the victim's status is also separated from the previous criminal proceedings, without prejudice to the work reserved for jurisdiction and without prejudice to the general rule of the connection to the prevalence of the judicial truth and the principle of the tried thing. Thank you very much, Madam President; thank you very much, ladies and gentlemen.
